href="/wiki/File:HARRISON-MORTON_INAUGURATION_RIBBON.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/6/67/HARRISON-MORTON_INAUGURATION_RIBBON.jpg/102px-HARRISON-MORTON_INAUGURATION_RIBBON.jpg" decoding="async" width="102" height="191" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/6/67/HARRISON-MORTON_INAUGURATION_RIBBON.jpg/153px-HARRISON-MORTON_INAUGURATION_RIBBON.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/6/67/HARRISON-MORTON_INAUGURATION_RIBBON.jpg/204px-HARRISON-MORTON_INAUGURATION_RIBBON.jpg 2x" data-file-width="533" data-file-height="1000" /></a></span></div><div class="thumbcaption">Souvenir ribbon with portraits</div></div></div></div></div>
<p>Harrison was sworn into office on Monday, March 4, 1889, by <a href="/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_the_United_States" title="Chief Justice of the United States">Chief Justice</a> <a href="/wiki/Melville_Fuller" title="Melville Fuller">Melville Fuller</a>. His speech was brief—half as long as that of his grandfather, William Henry Harrison, whose speech remains the longest inaugural address of a U.S. president.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter1–2_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter1–2-108">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> In his speech, Benjamin Harrison credited the nation's growth to the influences of education and religion, urged the cotton states and mining territories to attain the industrial proportions of the eastern states and promised a protective tariff. Of commerce, he said, "If our great corporations would more scrupulously observe their legal obligations and duties, they would have less call to complain of the limitations of their rights or of interference with their operations."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter3_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter3-109">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> Harrison also urged early statehood for the territories and advocated pensions for veterans, a call that met with enthusiastic applause. In foreign affairs, Harrison reaffirmed the <a href="/wiki/Monroe_Doctrine" title="Monroe Doctrine">Monroe Doctrine</a> as a mainstay of foreign policy, while urging modernization of the Navy and a merchant marine force. He gave his commitment to international peace through noninterference in the affairs of foreign governments.<sup id="cite_ref-Benjamin_Harrison_Inauguration_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Benjamin_Harrison_Inauguration-110">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/John_Philip_Sousa" title="John Philip Sousa">John Philip Sousa</a>'s <a href="/wiki/United_States_Marine_Corps" title="United States Marine Corps">Marine Corps</a> band played at the Inaugural Ball inside the <a href="/wiki/National_Building_Museum" title="National Building Museum">Pension Building</a> with a large crowd attending.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter5–6_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter5–6-111">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> After moving into the White House, Harrison noted, quite prophetically, "There is only a door—one that is never locked—between the president's office and what are not very accurately called his private apartments. There should be an executive office building, not too far away, but wholly distinct from the dwelling house. For everyone else in the public service, there is an unroofed space between the bedroom and the desk."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter83_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter83-112">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Harrison-inauguration_(edit).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Harrison-inauguration_%28edit%29.jpg/200px-Harrison-inauguration_%28edit%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Harrison-inauguration_%28edit%29.jpg/300px-Harrison-inauguration_%28edit%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Harrison-inauguration_%28edit%29.jpg/400px-Harrison-inauguration_%28edit%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4173" data-file-height="3450" /></a><figcaption>Inauguration of Benjamin Harrison, March 4, 1889. Cleveland held Harrison's umbrella.</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Puck_cover_8_May_1889_President_Harrison.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/bb/Puck_cover_8_May_1889_President_Harrison.jpg/220px-Puck_cover_8_May_1889_President_Harrison.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="299" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/bb/Puck_cover_8_May_1889_President_Harrison.jpg/330px-Puck_cover_8_May_1889_President_Harrison.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/bb/Puck_cover_8_May_1889_President_Harrison.jpg/440px-Puck_cover_8_May_1889_President_Harrison.jpg 2x" data-file-width="931" data-file-height="1266" /></a><figcaption>Harrison hounded by office seekers at the beginning of his term, May 1889, from <a href="/wiki/Puck_(magazine)" title="Puck (magazine)"><i>Puck</i></a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Harrison acted quite independently in selecting his cabinet, much to the Republican bosses' dismay. He began by delaying the presumed nomination of James G. Blaine as Secretary of State so as to preclude Blaine's involvement in the formation of the administration, as had occurred in President Garfield's term.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter20–22_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter20–22-113">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> In fact, other than Blaine, the only Republican boss initially nominated was Redfield Proctor, as Secretary of War. Senator Shelby Cullom's comment symbolizes Harrison's steadfast aversion to use federal positions for patronage: "I suppose Harrison treated me as well as he did any other Senator; but whenever he did anything for me, it was done so ungraciously that the concession tended to anger rather than please."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter33_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter33-114">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> Harrison's selections shared particular alliances, such as their service in the Civil War, Indiana citizenship and membership in the Presbyterian Church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter20_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter20-115">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, Harrison had alienated pivotal Republican operatives from New York to Pennsylvania to Iowa with these choices and prematurely compromised his political power and future.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter22–30_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter22–30-116">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> His normal schedule provided for two full cabinet meetings per week, as well as separate weekly one-on-one meetings with each cabinet member.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter85_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter85-117">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In June 1890, Harrison's Postmaster General <a href="/wiki/John_Wanamaker" title="John Wanamaker">John Wanamaker</a> and several Philadelphia friends purchased a large new cottage at <a href="/wiki/Cape_May_Point,_New_Jersey" title="Cape May Point, New Jersey">Cape May Point</a> for Harrison's wife, <a href="/wiki/Caroline_Harrison" title="Caroline Harrison">Caroline</a>. Many believed the cottage gift appeared improper and amounted to a bribe for a cabinet position. Harrison made no comment on the matter for two weeks, then said he had always intended to purchase the cottage once Caroline approved. On July 2, perhaps a little tardily to avoid suspicion, Harrison gave Wanamaker a check for $10,000 (equivalent to $325,704&#32;in 2022) for the cottage.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilliams194_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams194-118">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Civil_service_reform_and_pensions">Civil service reform and pensions</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Benjamin_Harrison_by_Eastman_Johnson.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Benjamin_Harrison_by_Eastman_Johnson.png/170px-Benjamin_Harrison_by_Eastman_Johnson.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="210" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Benjamin_Harrison_by_Eastman_Johnson.png/255px-Benjamin_Harrison_by_Eastman_Johnson.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Benjamin_Harrison_by_Eastman_Johnson.png/340px-Benjamin_Harrison_by_Eastman_Johnson.png 2x" data-file-width="412" data-file-height="510" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Eastman_Johnson" title="Eastman Johnson">Eastman Johnson</a>'s portrait of Benjamin Harrison, c. 1890–1900</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Civil_service" title="Civil service">Civil service</a> reform was a prominent issue following Harrison's election. Harrison had campaigned as a supporter of the <a href="/wiki/Merit_system" title="Merit system">merit system</a>, as opposed to the <a href="/wiki/Spoils_system" title="Spoils system">spoils system</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter32_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter32-119">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> Although some of the civil service had been classified under the <a href="/wiki/Pendleton_Civil_Service_Reform_Act" title="Pendleton Civil Service Reform Act">Pendleton Act</a> by previous administrations, Harrison spent much of his first months in office deciding on political appointments.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter32–36_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter32–36-120">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> Congress was widely divided on the issue and Harrison was reluctant to address the issue in hope of preventing the alienation of either side. The issue became a <a href="/wiki/Political_football" title="Political football">political football</a> of the time and was immortalized in a cartoon captioned "What can I do when both parties insist on kicking?"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMooreHale200683,_86_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMooreHale200683,_86-121">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> Harrison appointed <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt" title="Theodore Roosevelt">Theodore Roosevelt</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hugh_Smith_Thompson" title="Hugh Smith Thompson">Hugh Smith Thompson</a>, both reformers, to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Civil_Service_Commission" title="United States Civil Service Commission">Civil Service Commission</a>, but otherwise did little to further the reform cause.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter39–41_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter39–41-122">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Harrison_Football_Political_Cartoon.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/42/Harrison_Football_Political_Cartoon.jpg/220px-Harrison_Football_Political_Cartoon.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="136" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/42/Harrison_Football_Political_Cartoon.jpg/330px-Harrison_Football_Political_Cartoon.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/42/Harrison_Football_Political_Cartoon.jpg 2x" data-file-width="358" data-file-height="222" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Political_football" title="Political football">Political football</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Harrison quickly saw the enactment of the <a href="/wiki/Dependent_and_Disability_Pension_Act" title="Dependent and Disability Pension Act">Dependent and Disability Pension Act</a> in 1890, a cause he had championed while in Congress. In addition to providing pensions to disabled Civil War veterans (regardless of the cause of their disability), the Act depleted some of the troublesome federal budget surplus. Pension expenditures reached $135&#160;million under Harrison (equivalent to $4.4&#160;billion in 2022), the largest expenditure of its kind to that point in American history, a problem exacerbated by <a href="/wiki/Pension_Bureau" class="mw-redirect" title="Pension Bureau">Pension Bureau</a> commissioner <a href="/wiki/James_R._Tanner" title="James R. Tanner">James R. Tanner</a>'s expansive interpretation of the pension laws.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter36–37Calhoun200572–73_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter36–37Calhoun200572–73-123">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> An investigation into the Pension Bureau by Harrison's Secretary of Interior <a href="/wiki/John_Willock_Noble" title="John Willock Noble">John Willock Noble</a> found evidence of lavish and illegal handouts under Tanner.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilliams193_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams193-124">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> Harrison, who privately believed that appointing Tanner had been a mistake, due to his apparent loose management style and tongue, asked Tanner to resign and replaced him with <a href="/wiki/Green_Berry_Raum" title="Green Berry Raum">Green B. Raum</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter34–36_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter34–36-125">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> Raum was also accused of accepting loan payments in return for expediting pension cases. Harrison, having accepted a dissenting congressional Republican investigation report that exonerated Raum, kept him in office for the rest of his administration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilliams193–194_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams193–194-126">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>One of the first appointments Harrison was forced to reverse was that of James S. Clarkson as an assistant postmaster. Clarkson, who had expected a full cabinet position, began sabotaging the appointment from the outset, gaining the reputation for "decapitating a fourth class postmaster every three minutes". Clarkson himself stated, "I am simply on detail from the Republican Committee&#160;... I am most anxious to get through this task and leave." He resigned in September 1890.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter34–36_125-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter34–36-125">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Tariff">Tariff</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Billion_dollar_Congress.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Billion_dollar_Congress.jpg/170px-Billion_dollar_Congress.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="187" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Billion_dollar_Congress.jpg/255px-Billion_dollar_Congress.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Billion_dollar_Congress.jpg/340px-Billion_dollar_Congress.jpg 2x" data-file-width="623" data-file-height="685" /></a><figcaption>Harrison and the <a href="/wiki/51st_United_States_Congress" title="51st United States Congress">Billion-Dollar Congress</a> are portrayed as wasting the surplus in this cartoon from <i><a href="/wiki/Puck_(magazine)" title="Puck (magazine)">Puck</a></i>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Tariff" title="Tariff">tariff</a> levels had been a major political issue since before the Civil War, and they became the most dominant matter of the 1888 election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter51_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter51-127">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> The high tariff rates had created a surplus of money in the Treasury, which led many Democrats (as well as the growing Populist movement) to call for lowering them. Most Republicans preferred to maintain the rates, spend the surplus on <a href="/wiki/Public_works" title="Public works">internal improvements</a> and eliminate some internal taxes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter49_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter49-128">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Representative <a href="/wiki/William_McKinley" title="William McKinley">William McKinley</a> and Senator <a href="/wiki/Nelson_W._Aldrich" title="Nelson W. Aldrich">Nelson W. Aldrich</a> framed the <a href="/wiki/McKinley_Tariff" title="McKinley Tariff">McKinley Tariff</a> that would raise the tariff even higher, including making some rates intentionally prohibitive.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005100–104Socolofsky_&amp;_Spetter51–52_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005100–104Socolofsky_&amp;_Spetter51–52-129">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> At Secretary of State James Blaine's urging, Harrison attempted to make the tariff more acceptable by urging Congress to add <a href="/wiki/Reciprocity_(international_relations)" title="Reciprocity (international relations)">reciprocity</a> provisions, which would allow the president to reduce rates when other countries reduced their rates on American exports.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter51_127-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter51-127">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> The tariff was removed from imported raw <a href="/wiki/Sugar" title="Sugar">sugar</a>, and sugar growers in the United States were given a two cent per pound subsidy on their production.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005100–104Socolofsky_&amp;_Spetter51–52_129-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005100–104Socolofsky_&amp;_Spetter51–52-129">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> Even with the reductions and reciprocity, the McKinley Tariff enacted the highest average rate in American history, and the spending associated with it contributed to the reputation of the <a href="/wiki/51st_United_States_Congress" title="51st United States Congress">Billion-Dollar Congress</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter51_127-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter51-127">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Antitrust_laws_and_the_currency">Antitrust laws and the currency</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John-Sherman-2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/John-Sherman-2.jpg/170px-John-Sherman-2.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="205" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/John-Sherman-2.jpg/255px-John-Sherman-2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/John-Sherman-2.jpg/340px-John-Sherman-2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2986" data-file-height="3595" /></a><figcaption>Senator <a href="/wiki/John_Sherman" title="John Sherman">John Sherman</a> worked closely with Harrison, writing bills regulating <a href="/wiki/Sherman_Antitrust_Act" title="Sherman Antitrust Act">monopolies</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sherman_Silver_Purchase_Act" title="Sherman Silver Purchase Act">monetary policy</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Members of both parties were concerned with the growth of the power of <a href="/wiki/Trust_(19th_century)" class="mw-redirect" title="Trust (19th century)">trusts</a> and <a href="/wiki/Monopoly" title="Monopoly">monopolies</a>, and one of the first acts of the <a href="/wiki/51st_United_States_Congress" title="51st United States Congress">51st Congress</a> was to pass the <a href="/wiki/Sherman_Antitrust_Act" title="Sherman Antitrust Act">Sherman Antitrust Act</a>, sponsored by Senator <a href="/wiki/John_Sherman" title="John Sherman">John Sherman</a> of Ohio. The Act passed by wide margins in both houses, and Harrison signed it into law.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter53_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter53-130">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> The Sherman Act was the first Federal act of its kind, and marked a new use of federal government power.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun200592–93_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun200592–93-131">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> While Harrison approved of the law and its intent, his administration was not particularly vigorous in enforcing it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter54Calhoun200594_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter54Calhoun200594-132">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> However, the government successfully concluded a case during Harrison's time in office (against a Tennessee coal company),<sup id="cite_ref-coal_case_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-coal_case-133">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> and had initiated several other cases against trusts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter54Calhoun200594_132-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter54Calhoun200594-132">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>One of the most volatile questions of the 1880s was whether the currency should be backed by <a href="/wiki/Bimetallism" title="Bimetallism">gold and silver</a>, or by <a href="/wiki/Gold_standard" title="Gold standard">gold alone</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun200594–95_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun200594–95-134">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> The issue cut across party lines, with western Republicans and southern Democrats joining together in the call for the free coinage of silver, and both parties' representatives in the northeast holding firm for the gold standard. Because silver was worth less than its legal equivalent in gold, taxpayers paid their government bills in silver, while international creditors demanded payment in gold, resulting in a depletion of the nation's gold supply. Owing to worldwide <a href="/wiki/Deflation" title="Deflation">deflation</a> in the late 19th century, however, a strict gold standard had resulted in reduction of incomes without the equivalent reduction in debts, pushing debtors and the poor to call for silver coinage as an inflationary measure.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun200594–95Socolofsky_&amp;_Spetter55–59_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun200594–95Socolofsky_&amp;_Spetter55–59-135">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The silver coinage issue had not been much discussed in the 1888 campaign and Harrison is said to have favored a bimetallist position.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter53_130-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter53-130">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> However, his appointment of a silverite Treasury Secretary, <a href="/wiki/William_Windom" title="William Windom">William Windom</a>, encouraged the free silver supporters.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter56–57_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter56–57-136">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> Harrison attempted to steer a middle course between the two positions, advocating a free coinage of silver, but at its own value, not at a fixed ratio to gold.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter58Calhoun200596_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter58Calhoun200596-137">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> This failed to facilitate a compromise between the factions. In July 1890, Senator Sherman achieved passage of a bill, the <a href="/wiki/Sherman_Silver_Purchase_Act" title="Sherman Silver Purchase Act">Sherman Silver Purchase Act</a>, in both houses.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter58Calhoun200596_137-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter58Calhoun200596-137">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> Harrison thought that the bill would end the controversy, and he signed it into law.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter59_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter59-138">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> The effect of the bill, however, was the increased depletion of the nation's gold supply, a problem that would persist until the second Cleveland administration <a href="/wiki/Grover_Cleveland#Economic_panic_and_the_silver_issue" title="Grover Cleveland">resolved it</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter60_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter60-139">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Civil_rights">Civil rights</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:President_Benjamin_Harrison_on_ship.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/President_Benjamin_Harrison_on_ship.jpg/220px-President_Benjamin_Harrison_on_ship.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="157" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/President_Benjamin_Harrison_on_ship.jpg/330px-President_Benjamin_Harrison_on_ship.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/President_Benjamin_Harrison_on_ship.jpg/440px-President_Benjamin_Harrison_on_ship.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1180" data-file-height="842" /></a><figcaption>Harrison with Secretary Blaine and Representative <a href="/wiki/Henry_Cabot_Lodge" title="Henry Cabot Lodge">Henry Cabot Lodge</a> off the coast of <a href="/wiki/Maine" title="Maine">Maine</a>, 1889</figcaption></figure>
<p>After regaining the majority in both Houses of Congress, some Republicans, led by Harrison, attempted to pass legislation to protect black Americans' civil rights. Harrison's Attorney General, <a href="/wiki/William_H._H._Miller" title="William H. H. Miller">William H. H. Miller</a>, through the Justice Department, ordered the prosecutions for violation of voting rights in the South; however, white juries often failed to convict or indict violators. This prompted Harrison to urge Congress to pass legislation that would "secure all our people a free exercise of the right of suffrage and every other civil right under the Constitution and laws".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun200589–90_140-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun200589–90-140">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> Harrison endorsed the proposed <a href="/wiki/Lodge_Bill" title="Lodge Bill">Federal Elections Bill</a> written by Representative <a href="/wiki/Henry_Cabot_Lodge" title="Henry Cabot Lodge">Henry Cabot Lodge</a> and Senator <a href="/wiki/George_Frisbie_Hoar" class="mw-redirect" title="George Frisbie Hoar">George Frisbie Hoar</a> in 1890, but the bill was defeated in the Senate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson32–33_141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson32–33-141">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> Following the failure to pass the bill, Harrison continued to speak in favor of <a href="/wiki/African_American" class="mw-redirect" title="African American">African American</a> civil rights in addresses to Congress. Most notably, on December 3, 1889, Harrison had gone before Congress and stated:
</p>
<blockquote><p>The colored people did not intrude themselves upon us; they were brought here in chains and held in communities where they are now chiefly bound by a cruel slave code...when and under what conditions is the black man to have a free ballot? When is he in fact to have those full civil rights which have so long been his in law? When is that quality of influence which our form of government was intended to secure to the electors to be restored? … in many parts of our country where the colored population is large the people of that race are by various devices deprived of any effective exercise of their political rights and of many of their civil rights. The wrong does not expend itself upon those whose votes are suppressed. Every constituency in the Union is wronged.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter62_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter62-142">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>He severely questioned the states' civil rights records, arguing that if states have the authority over civil rights, then "we have a right to ask whether they are at work upon it."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilson32–33_141-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson32–33-141">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> Harrison also supported a bill proposed by Senator <a href="/wiki/Henry_W._Blair" title="Henry W. Blair">Henry W. Blair</a>, which would have granted federal funding to schools regardless of the students' races.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter65–67_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter65–67-143">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> He also endorsed a proposed constitutional amendment to overturn the Supreme Court ruling in the <i><a href="/wiki/Civil_Rights_Cases" title="Civil Rights Cases">Civil Rights Cases</a></i> (1883) that declared much of the <a href="/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1875" title="Civil Rights Act of 1875">Civil Rights Act of 1875</a> unconstitutional. None of these measures gained congressional approval.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun200589–90Smith170_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun200589–90Smith170-144">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="National_forests">National forests</span></h3>
<p>In March 1891 Congress enacted, and Harrison signed, the <a href="/wiki/Land_Revision_Act_of_1891" class="mw-redirect" title="Land Revision Act of 1891">Land Revision Act of 1891</a>. This legislation resulted from a bipartisan desire to initiate <a href="/wiki/Land_reclamation" title="Land reclamation">reclamation</a> of surplus lands that had been, up to that point, granted from the public domain, for potential settlement or use by railroad syndicates. As the law's drafting was finalized, Section 24 was added at the behest of Harrison by his Secretary of the Interior John Noble, which read as follows:
</p>
<blockquote><p>That the President of the United States may, from time to time, set apart and reserve, in any State or Territory having public land bearing forests, in any part of the public lands wholly or in part covered with timber or undergrowth, whether of commercial value or not, as public reservations, and the president shall, by public proclamation, declare the establishment of such reservations and the limits thereof.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter71_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter71-145">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Within a month of the enactment of this law Harrison authorized the first forest reserve, to be located on public domain adjacent to <a href="/wiki/Yellowstone_National_Park" title="Yellowstone National Park">Yellowstone National Park</a>, in Wyoming. Other areas were so designated by Harrison, bringing the first forest reservations total to 22&#160;million acres in his term.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter72_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter72-146">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> Harrison was also the first to give a prehistoric Indian Ruin, <a href="/wiki/Casa_Grande_Ruins_National_Monument" title="Casa Grande Ruins National Monument">Casa Grande</a> in Arizona, federal protection.<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Native_American_policy">Native American policy</span></h3>
<p>During Harrison's administration, the <a href="/wiki/Lakota_people" title="Lakota people">Lakota</a>, who had been forcibly confined to <a href="/wiki/Indian_reservation" title="Indian reservation">reservations</a> in <a href="/wiki/South_Dakota" title="South Dakota">South Dakota</a>, grew restive under the influence of <a href="/wiki/Wovoka" title="Wovoka">Wovoka</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Medicine_man" title="Medicine man">medicine man</a>, who encouraged them to participate in a spiritual movement known as the <a href="/wiki/Ghost_Dance" title="Ghost Dance">Ghost Dance</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter106_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter106-148">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> Though the movement called for the removal of <a href="/wiki/White_Americans" title="White Americans">white Americans</a> from indigenous lands, it was primarily religious in nature, a fact which many in Washington did not understand; assuming that the Ghost Dance would increase Lakota resistance to U.S. government, they ordered the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Armed_Forces" title="United States Armed Forces">American military</a> to increase their presence on the reservations. On December 29, 1890, the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Army" title="United States Army">U.S. Army</a>'s <a href="/wiki/7th_Cavalry_Regiment" title="7th Cavalry Regiment">7th Cavalry Regiment</a> perpetrated a <a href="/wiki/Wounded_Knee_Massacre" title="Wounded Knee Massacre">massacre of over 250 Lakota</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Pine_Ridge_Indian_Reservation" title="Pine Ridge Indian Reservation">Pine Ridge Indian Reservation</a> near <a href="/wiki/Wounded_Knee_Creek" title="Wounded Knee Creek">Wounded Knee Creek</a> after a botched attempt to disarm the reservation's inhabitants. The massacre's victims, many of whom were women and children, were buried in <a href="/wiki/Mass_grave" title="Mass grave">mass graves</a> by American soldiers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006121–122_149-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006121–122-149">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter106–107_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter106–107-150">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>In response to the massacre, Harrison directed Major-General <a href="/wiki/Nelson_A._Miles" title="Nelson A. Miles">Nelson A. Miles</a> to investigate and ordered 3,500 U.S. troops to be <a href="/wiki/Ghost_Dance_War" title="Ghost Dance War">deployed to South Dakota</a>, which suppressed the Ghost Dance movement.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter106_148-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter106-148">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> The massacre has been widely considered as the last major engagement of the <a href="/wiki/American_Indian_Wars" title="American Indian Wars">American Indian Wars</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006121–122_149-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006121–122-149">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> Harrison's general policy on <a href="/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States" title="Native Americans in the United States">Native Americans in the United States</a> was to encourage their <a href="/wiki/Cultural_assimilation_of_Native_Americans" title="Cultural assimilation of Native Americans">assimilation</a> into white society and, despite the massacre, he believed the policy to have been generally successful.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter92_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter92-151">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> This policy, known as the allotment system and embodied in the <a href="/wiki/Dawes_Act" title="Dawes Act">Dawes Act</a>, was favored by liberal reformers at the time, but eventually proved detrimental to Native Americans as they sold most of their land at low prices to white speculators.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005112–114Stuart452–454_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005112–114Stuart452–454-152">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Technology_and_naval_modernization">Technology and naval modernization</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:USS_Texas2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/USS_Texas2.jpg/220px-USS_Texas2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="163" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/USS_Texas2.jpg/330px-USS_Texas2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/USS_Texas2.jpg/440px-USS_Texas2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="7147" data-file-height="5290" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/USS_Texas_(1892)" title="USS Texas (1892)">USS&#160;<i>Texas</i></a>, America's first battleship, built in 1892</figcaption></figure><p>
During Harrison's time in office, the United States was continuing to experience advances in science and technology. A recording of his voice is the earliest extant recording of a president while he was in office. That </p><span data-nosnippet="" id="ooui-php-1" class="ext-phonos ext-phonos-PhonosButton noexcerpt oo-ui-widget oo-ui-widget-enabled oo-ui-buttonElement oo-ui-buttonElement-frameless oo-ui-iconElement oo-ui-labelElement oo-ui-buttonWidget" data-ooui="{&quot;_&quot;:&quot;mw.Phonos.PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;\/\/upload.wikimedia.org\/wikipedia\/commons\/transcoded\/9\/95\/Benjamin_Harrison_speech.ogg\/Benjamin_Harrison_speech.ogg.mp3&quot;,&quot;rel&quot;:[&quot;nofollow&quot;],&quot;framed&quot;:false,&quot;icon&quot;:&quot;volumeUp&quot;,&quot;label&quot;:{&quot;html&quot;:&quot;&lt;pre&gt;thirty-six-second recording\n&lt;\/pre&gt;&quot;},&quot;data&quot;:{&quot;ipa&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;text&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;en&quot;,&quot;wikibase&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;file&quot;:&quot;Benjamin Harrison speech.ogg&quot;},&quot;classes&quot;:[&quot;ext-phonos&quot;,&quot;ext-phonos-PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;noexcerpt&quot;]}"><a role="button" tabindex="0" href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/9/95/Benjamin_Harrison_speech.ogg/Benjamin_Harrison_speech.ogg.mp3" rel="nofollow" aria-label="Play audio" class="oo-ui-buttonElement-button"><span class="oo-ui-iconElement-icon oo-ui-icon-volumeUp"></span><span class="oo-ui-labelElement-label"><pre>thirty-six-second recording
</pre></span><span class="oo-ui-indicatorElement-indicator oo-ui-indicatorElement-noIndicator"></span></a></span><p><sup class="ext-phonos-attribution noexcerpt"><a href="/wiki/File:Benjamin_Harrison_speech.ogg" title="File:Benjamin Harrison speech.ogg">i</a></sup> was originally made on a wax <a href="/wiki/Phonograph_cylinder" title="Phonograph cylinder">phonograph cylinder</a> in 1889 by <a href="/wiki/Gianni_Bettini" title="Gianni Bettini">Gianni Bettini</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Vincent_Voice_Library_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Vincent_Voice_Library-153">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> Harrison also had electricity installed in the White House for the first time by <a href="/wiki/General_Electric" title="General Electric">Edison General Electric Company</a>, but he and his wife would not touch the light switches for fear of electrocution and would often go to sleep with the lights on.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMooreHale200696_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMooreHale200696-154">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Over the course of his administration, Harrison marshaled the country's technology to clothe the nation with a credible naval power. When he took office there were only two commissioned warships in the Navy. In his inaugural address he said, "construction of a sufficient number of warships and their necessary armaments should progress as rapidly as is consistent with care and perfection."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter97_155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter97-155">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> Harrison's Secretary of the Navy <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_F._Tracy" title="Benjamin F. Tracy">Benjamin F. Tracy</a> spearheaded the rapid construction of vessels, and within a year congressional approval was obtained for building of the warships <a href="/wiki/USS_Indiana_(BB-1)" title="USS Indiana (BB-1)"><i>Indiana</i></a>, <a href="/wiki/USS_Texas_(1892)" title="USS Texas (1892)"><i>Texas</i></a>, <a href="/wiki/USS_Oregon_(BB-3)" title="USS Oregon (BB-3)"><i>Oregon</i></a>, and <a href="/wiki/USS_Columbia_(C-12)" title="USS Columbia (C-12)"><i>Columbia</i></a>. By 1898, with the help of the Carnegie Corporation, no less than ten modern warships, including steel hulls and greater displacements and armaments, had transformed the United States into a legitimate naval power. Seven of these had begun during the Harrison term.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter102_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter102-156">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Latin_America_and_Samoa">Latin America and Samoa</span></h4>
<p>Harrison and <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_State" title="United States Secretary of State">Secretary of State</a> Blaine were often not the most cordial of friends, but harmonized in an aggressive foreign policy and commercial reciprocity with other nations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun200574–76_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun200574–76-157">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> Blaine's persistent medical problems warranted more of a hands-on effort by Harrison in the conduct of foreign policy. In <a href="/wiki/San_Francisco" title="San Francisco">San Francisco</a>, while on tour of the United States in 1891, Harrison proclaimed that the United States was in a "new epoch" of trade and that the expanding navy would protect oceanic shipping and increase American influence and prestige abroad.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005119–121_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005119–121-158">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/First_International_Conference_of_American_States" title="First International Conference of American States">First International Conference of American States</a> met in <a href="/wiki/Washington,_D.C." title="Washington, D.C.">Washington</a> in 1889; Harrison set an aggressive agenda including customs and currency integration and named a bipartisan delegation to the conference, led by <a href="/wiki/John_B._Henderson" title="John B. Henderson">John B. Henderson</a> and <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Carnegie" title="Andrew Carnegie">Andrew Carnegie</a>. The conference failed to achieve any diplomatic breakthrough, due in large part to an atmosphere of suspicion fostered by the Argentinian delegation. It did succeed in establishing an information center that became the <a href="/wiki/Organization_of_American_States" title="Organization of American States">Pan American Union</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006108_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006108-159">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> In response to the diplomatic bust, Harrison and Blaine pivoted diplomatically and initiated a crusade for tariff reciprocity with Latin American nations; the Harrison administration concluded eight reciprocity treaties among these countries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter117–120_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter117–120-160">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> On another front, Harrison sent <a href="/wiki/Frederick_Douglass" title="Frederick Douglass">Frederick Douglass</a> as ambassador to <a href="/wiki/Haiti" title="Haiti">Haiti</a>, but failed in his attempts to establish a naval base there.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter126–128_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter126–128-161">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1889, the United States, the United Kingdom, and the <a href="/wiki/German_Empire" title="German Empire">German Empire</a> were locked in a dispute over control of the Samoan Islands. Historian George H. Ryden's research indicates Harrison played a key role in determining the status of this Pacific outpost by taking a firm stand on every aspect of Samoa conference negotiations; this included selection of the local ruler, refusal to allow an indemnity for Germany, as well as the establishment of a three power protectorate, a first for the U.S.. These arrangements facilitated the future dominant power of the U.S. in the Pacific; Secretary of State Blaine was absent due to complication of <a href="/wiki/Low_back_pain" title="Low back pain">lumbago</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter115–116_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter115–116-162">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="European_embargo_of_U.S._pork">European embargo of U.S. pork</span></h4>
<p>Throughout the 1880s various European countries had imposed a ban on importation of United States pork out of an unconfirmed concern of <a href="/wiki/Trichinosis" title="Trichinosis">trichinosis</a>; at issue was over one billion pounds of pork products with a value of $80&#160;million annually (equivalent to $2.6&#160;billion in 2022). Harrison engaged Whitelaw Reid, minister to France, and <a href="/wiki/William_Walter_Phelps" title="William Walter Phelps">William Walter Phelps</a>, minister to Germany, to restore these exports for the country without delay. Harrison also successfully asked the congress to enact the Meat Inspection Act to eliminate the accusations of product compromise. The president also partnered with Agriculture Secretary Rusk to threaten Germany with retaliation – by initiating an embargo in the U.S. against Germany's highly demanded beet sugar. By September 1891 Germany relented, and was soon followed by Denmark, France and Austria-Hungary.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter131–136_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter131–136-163">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Crises_in_Aleutian_Islands_and_Chile">Crises in Aleutian Islands and Chile</span></h4>
<p>The first international crisis Harrison faced arose from disputed fishing rights on the <a href="/wiki/Alaska" title="Alaska">Alaskan</a> coast. <a href="/wiki/Canada" title="Canada">Canada</a> claimed fishing and <a href="/wiki/Seal_hunting" title="Seal hunting">sealing</a> rights around many of the <a href="/wiki/Aleutian_Islands" title="Aleutian Islands">Aleutian Islands</a>, in violation of U.S. law. As a result, the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Navy" title="United States Navy">United States Navy</a> seized several Canadian ships.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter137–138_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter137–138-164">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> In 1891, the administration began negotiations with the British that would eventually lead to a compromise over fishing rights after international arbitration, with the British government paying compensation in 1898.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006135–136_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006135–136-165">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter139–143_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter139–143-166">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1891, a diplomatic crisis emerged in <a href="/wiki/Chile" title="Chile">Chile</a>, otherwise known as the <a href="/wiki/Baltimore_Crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="Baltimore Crisis"><i>Baltimore</i> Crisis</a>. The American minister to Chile, <a href="/wiki/Patrick_Egan_(land_reformer_and_diplomat)" class="mw-redirect" title="Patrick Egan (land reformer and diplomat)">Patrick Egan</a>, granted asylum to Chileans who were seeking refuge during the <a href="/wiki/1891_Chilean_Civil_War" class="mw-redirect" title="1891 Chilean Civil War">1891 Chilean Civil War</a>. Egan, previously a militant Irish immigrant to the U.S., was motivated by a personal desire to thwart Great Britain's influence in Chile;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter146_167-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter146-167">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> his action increased tensions between Chile and the United States, which began in the early 1880s when Secretary Blaine had alienated the Chileans in the <a href="/wiki/War_of_the_Pacific" title="War of the Pacific">War of the Pacific</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Uss_baltimore_c-3.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Uss_baltimore_c-3.jpg/220px-Uss_baltimore_c-3.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="116" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Uss_baltimore_c-3.jpg/330px-Uss_baltimore_c-3.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Uss_baltimore_c-3.jpg/440px-Uss_baltimore_c-3.jpg 2x" data-file-width="740" data-file-height="391" /></a><figcaption>Attack on sailors from <a href="/wiki/USS_Baltimore_(C-3)" title="USS Baltimore (C-3)">USS&#160;<i>Baltimore</i></a> spawned the 1891 Chilean crisis.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The crisis began in earnest when sailors from <a href="/wiki/USS_Baltimore_(C-3)" title="USS Baltimore (C-3)">USS&#160;<i>Baltimore</i></a> took <a href="/wiki/Shore_leave" title="Shore leave">shore leave</a> in <a href="/wiki/Valparaiso" class="mw-redirect" title="Valparaiso">Valparaiso</a> and a fight ensued, resulting in the deaths of two American sailors and the arrest of three dozen others.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005127_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005127-168">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> <i>Baltimore</i><span class="nowrap" style="padding-left:0.1em;">&#39;</span>s captain, Winfield Schley, based on the nature of the sailors' wounds, insisted the sailors had been bayonet-attacked by Chilean police without provocation. With Blaine incapacitated, Harrison drafted a demand for reparations. The Chilean Minister of Foreign Affairs Manuel Matta replied that Harrison's message was "erroneous or deliberately incorrect," and said that the Chilean government was treating the affair the same as any other criminal matter.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005128–129Socolofsky_&amp;_Spetter147–149_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005128–129Socolofsky_&amp;_Spetter147–149-169">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tensions increased to the brink of war – Harrison threatened to break off diplomatic relations unless the United States received a suitable apology, and said the situation required, "grave and patriotic consideration". The president also remarked, "If the dignity as well as the prestige and influence of the United States are not to be wholly sacrificed, we must protect those who in foreign ports display the flag or wear the colors."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter151_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter151-170">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> The Navy was also placed on a high level of preparedness.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005128–129Socolofsky_&amp;_Spetter147–149_169-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005128–129Socolofsky_&amp;_Spetter147–149-169">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> A recuperated Blaine made brief conciliatory overtures to the Chilean government which had no support in the administration; he then reversed course, joined the chorus for unconditional concessions and apology by the Chileans, who ultimately obliged, and war was averted. Theodore Roosevelt later applauded Harrison for his use of the "<a href="/wiki/Big_stick" class="mw-redirect" title="Big stick">big stick</a>" in the matter.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006134_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006134-171">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter150–151_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter150–151-172">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Annexation_of_Hawaii">Annexation of Hawaii</span></h4>
<p>In the last days of his administration, Harrison dealt with the issue of <a href="/wiki/Hawaii" title="Hawaii">Hawaiian</a> annexation. Following <a href="/wiki/Overthrow_of_the_Kingdom_of_Hawaii" class="mw-redirect" title="Overthrow of the Kingdom of Hawaii">a coup d'état</a> against Queen <a href="/wiki/Liliuokalani" class="mw-redirect" title="Liliuokalani">Liliuokalani</a>, the new government of Hawaii led by <a href="/wiki/Sanford_B._Dole" title="Sanford B. Dole">Sanford Dole</a> petitioned for annexation by the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter204–205_173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter204–205-173">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> Harrison was interested in expanding American influence in Hawaii and in establishing a naval base at <a href="/wiki/Pearl_Harbor" title="Pearl Harbor">Pearl Harbor</a> but had not previously expressed an opinion on annexing the islands.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005125–126_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005125–126-174">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> The United States <a href="/wiki/Consul_(representative)" title="Consul (representative)">consul</a> in Hawaii, <a href="/wiki/John_L._Stevens" title="John L. Stevens">John L. Stevens</a>, recognized the new government on February 1, 1893, and forwarded their proposals to Washington. With just one month left before leaving office, the administration signed a treaty on February 14 and submitted it to the Senate the next day with Harrison's recommendation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter204–205_173-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter204–205-173">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> The Senate failed to act, and President Cleveland withdrew the treaty shortly after taking office.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005132_175-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005132-175">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006147_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006147-176">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cabinet">Cabinet</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:BHarrison_cabinet.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/BHarrison_cabinet.jpg/220px-BHarrison_cabinet.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="171" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/BHarrison_cabinet.jpg/330px-BHarrison_cabinet.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/BHarrison_cabinet.jpg/440px-BHarrison_cabinet.jpg 2x" data-file-width="756" data-file-height="588" /></a><figcaption>Harrison's cabinet in 1889<br />Front row, left to right: <b>Harrison</b>, <a href="/wiki/William_Windom" title="William Windom">William Windom</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Wanamaker" title="John Wanamaker">John Wanamaker</a>, <a href="/wiki/Redfield_Proctor" title="Redfield Proctor">Redfield Proctor</a>, <a href="/wiki/James_G._Blaine" title="James G. Blaine">James G. Blaine</a><br />Back row, left to right: <a href="/wiki/William_H._H._Miller" title="William H. H. Miller">William H. H. Miller</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Willock_Noble" title="John Willock Noble">John Willock Noble</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jeremiah_M._Rusk" class="mw-redirect" title="Jeremiah M. Rusk">Jeremiah M. Rusk</a>, <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_F._Tracy" title="Benjamin F. Tracy">Benjamin F. Tracy</a></figcaption></figure>
<table class="infobox" style="width:auto;text-align:left;line-height:1.2em;margin-left:1em; margin-right:0; float:right; clear:right;"><tbody><tr><th colspan="3" style="line-height:1.5em;font-size:110%;background:#DCDCDC;text-align:center">The Harrison cabinet</th></tr><tr><th>Office</th><th>Name</th><th>Term</th></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#000"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">President</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><b>Benjamin Harrison</b></th><td>1889&#8211;1893</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/Vice_President_of_the_United_States" title="Vice President of the United States">Vice President</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Levi_P._Morton" title="Levi P.
