Morton</a></th><td>1889&#8211;1893</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td rowspan="2"><a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_State" title="United States Secretary of State">Secretary of State</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/James_G._Blaine" title="James G. Blaine">James G. Blaine</a></th><td>1889&#8211;1892</td></tr><tr class="mw-empty-elt"></tr><tr><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/John_W._Foster" title="John W. Foster">John W. Foster</a></th><td>1892&#8211;1893</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td rowspan="2"><a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Treasury" title="United States Secretary of the Treasury">Secretary of the Treasury</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/William_Windom" title="William Windom">William Windom</a></th><td>1889&#8211;1891</td></tr><tr class="mw-empty-elt"></tr><tr><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Charles_Foster_(Ohio_politician)" title="Charles Foster (Ohio politician)">Charles Foster</a></th><td>1891&#8211;1893</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td rowspan="2"><a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_War" title="United States Secretary of War">Secretary of War</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Redfield_Proctor" title="Redfield Proctor">Redfield Proctor</a></th><td>1889&#8211;1891</td></tr><tr class="mw-empty-elt"></tr><tr><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Stephen_Benton_Elkins" class="mw-redirect" title="Stephen Benton Elkins">Stephen Benton Elkins</a></th><td>1891&#8211;1893</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Attorney_General" title="United States Attorney General">Attorney General</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/William_H._H._Miller" title="William H. H. Miller">William H. H. Miller</a></th><td>1889&#8211;1893</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Postmaster_General" title="United States Postmaster General">Postmaster General</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/John_Wanamaker" title="John Wanamaker">John Wanamaker</a></th><td>1889&#8211;1893</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Navy" title="United States Secretary of the Navy">Secretary of the Navy</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Benjamin_F._Tracy" title="Benjamin F. Tracy">Benjamin F. Tracy</a></th><td>1889&#8211;1893</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Interior" title="United States Secretary of the Interior">Secretary of the Interior</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/John_Willock_Noble" title="John Willock Noble">John Willock Noble</a></th><td>1889&#8211;1893</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_Agriculture" title="United States Secretary of Agriculture">Secretary of Agriculture</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Jeremiah_McLain_Rusk" title="Jeremiah McLain Rusk">Jeremiah McLain Rusk</a></th><td>1889&#8211;1893</td></tr></tbody></table>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Judicial_appointments">Judicial appointments</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Harrison_judicial_appointments" class="mw-redirect" title="Benjamin Harrison judicial appointments">Benjamin Harrison judicial appointments</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:DavidBrewer.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/DavidBrewer.jpg/170px-DavidBrewer.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="230" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/DavidBrewer.jpg/255px-DavidBrewer.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/DavidBrewer.jpg/340px-DavidBrewer.jpg 2x" data-file-width="464" data-file-height="628" /></a><figcaption>Harrison appointed four Supreme Court justices, including <a href="/wiki/David_Josiah_Brewer" class="mw-redirect" title="David Josiah Brewer">David Josiah Brewer</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Harrison appointed four justices to the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States" title="Supreme Court of the United States">Supreme Court of the United States</a>. The first was <a href="/wiki/David_Josiah_Brewer" class="mw-redirect" title="David Josiah Brewer">David Josiah Brewer</a>, a judge on the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Eighth_Circuit" title="United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit">Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit</a>. Brewer, the nephew of Justice <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Johnson_Field" title="Stephen Johnson Field">Field</a>, had previously been considered for a cabinet position. Shortly after Brewer's nomination, Justice <a href="/wiki/Stanley_Matthews_(judge)" title="Stanley Matthews (judge)">Matthews</a> died, creating another vacancy. Harrison had considered <a href="/wiki/Henry_Billings_Brown" title="Henry Billings Brown">Henry Billings Brown</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Michigan" title="Michigan">Michigan</a> judge and <a href="/wiki/Admiralty_law" title="Admiralty law">admiralty law</a> expert, for the first vacancy and now nominated him for the second. For the third vacancy, which arose in 1892, Harrison nominated <a href="/wiki/George_Shiras_Jr." title="George Shiras Jr.">George Shiras</a>. Shiras's appointment was somewhat controversial because his age—sixty—was older than usual for a newly appointed Justice. Shiras also drew the opposition of Senator <a href="/wiki/Matthew_Quay" title="Matthew Quay">Matthew Quay</a> of Pennsylvania because they were in different factions of the Pennsylvania Republican party, but his nomination was nonetheless approved. Finally, at the end of his term, Harrison nominated <a href="/wiki/Howell_Edmunds_Jackson" class="mw-redirect" title="Howell Edmunds Jackson">Howell Edmunds Jackson</a> to replace Justice <a href="/wiki/Lucius_Quintus_Cincinnatus_Lamar_(II)" class="mw-redirect" title="Lucius Quintus Cincinnatus Lamar (II)">Lamar</a>, who died in January 1893. Harrison knew the incoming Senate would be controlled by Democrats, so he selected Jackson, a respected Tennessee Democrat with whom he was friendly to ensure his nominee would not be rejected. Jackson's nomination was indeed successful, but he died after only two years on the Court.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter188–190_177-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter188–190-177">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In addition to his Supreme Court appointments, Harrison appointed ten judges to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_court_of_appeals" class="mw-redirect" title="United States court of appeals">courts of appeals</a>, two judges to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_circuit_court" title="United States circuit court">circuit courts</a>, and 26 judges to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_district_court" title="United States district court">district courts</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="States_admitted_to_the_Union">States admitted to the Union</span></h3>
<p>Six new states were <a href="/wiki/Admission_to_the_Union" title="Admission to the Union">admitted to the Union</a> while Harrison was in office:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter44–45_178-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter44–45-178">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/North_Dakota" title="North Dakota">North Dakota</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32;<a href="/wiki/Enabling_Act_of_1889" title="Enabling Act of 1889">November 2, 1889</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/South_Dakota" title="South Dakota">South Dakota</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32;<a href="/wiki/Enabling_Act_of_1889" title="Enabling Act of 1889">November 2, 1889</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Montana" title="Montana">Montana</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32;<a href="/wiki/Enabling_Act_of_1889" title="Enabling Act of 1889">November 8, 1889</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Washington_(state)" title="Washington (state)">Washington</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32;<a href="/wiki/Enabling_Act_of_1889" title="Enabling Act of 1889">November 11, 1889</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Idaho" title="Idaho">Idaho</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32;July 3, 1890</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Wyoming" title="Wyoming">Wyoming</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32;July 10, 1890,</li></ul>
<p>More states were admitted during Harrison's presidency than any other.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Vacations_and_travel">Vacations and travel</span></h3>
<p>Harrison attended a grand, three-day <a href="/wiki/Legacy_of_George_Washington#Centennial_celebration" title="Legacy of George Washington">centennial celebration</a> of George Washington's inauguration in <a href="/wiki/New_York_City" title="New York City">New York City</a> on April 30, 1889, and made the following remarks "We have come into the serious but always inspiring presence of Washington. He was the incarnation of duty and he teaches us today this great lesson: that those who would associate their names with events that shall outlive a century can only do so by high consecration to duty. Self-seeking has no public observance or anniversary."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter160_179-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter160-179">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Harrisons made many trips out of the capital, which included speeches at most stops – including Philadelphia, New England, Indianapolis and Chicago. The President typically made his best impression speaking before large audiences, as opposed to more intimate settings.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter157_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter157-180">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> The most notable of his presidential trips, theretofore unequaled, was a five-week tour of the west in the spring of 1891, aboard a lavishly outfitted train.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter171_181-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter171-181">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> Harrison enjoyed a number of short trips out of the capital—usually for hunting—to nearby Virginia or Maryland.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter166_182-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter166-182">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the hot Washington summers, the Harrisons took refuge in <a href="/wiki/Deer_Park,_Maryland" title="Deer Park, Maryland">Deer Park, Maryland</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cape_May_Point,_New_Jersey" title="Cape May Point, New Jersey">Cape May Point, New Jersey</a>. In 1890, John Wanamaker joined with other Philadelphia devotees of the Harrisons and made a gift to them of a summer cottage at Cape May. Harrison, though appreciative, was uncomfortable with the appearance of impropriety; a month later, he paid Wanamaker $10,000 (equivalent to $325,704&#32;in 2022) as reimbursement to the donors. Nevertheless, Harrison's opponents made the gift the subject of national ridicule, and Mrs. Harrison and the president were vigorously criticized.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter168_183-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter168-183">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Reelection_campaign_in_1892">Reelection campaign in 1892</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1892_United_States_presidential_election" title="1892 United States presidential election">1892 United States presidential election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Eastman_Johnson_-_Benjamin_Harrison_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Eastman_Johnson_-_Benjamin_Harrison_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/130px-Eastman_Johnson_-_Benjamin_Harrison_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" decoding="async" width="130" height="196" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Eastman_Johnson_-_Benjamin_Harrison_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/195px-Eastman_Johnson_-_Benjamin_Harrison_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Eastman_Johnson_-_Benjamin_Harrison_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/260px-Eastman_Johnson_-_Benjamin_Harrison_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3502" data-file-height="5271" /></a><figcaption>Official <a href="/wiki/White_House" title="White House">White House</a> portrait of Benjamin Harrison, painted by <a href="/wiki/Eastman_Johnson" title="Eastman Johnson">Eastman Johnson</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The treasury surplus had evaporated and the nation's economic health was worsening – precursors to the eventual <a href="/wiki/Panic_of_1893" title="Panic of 1893">Panic of 1893</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005107,_126–127_184-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005107,_126–127-184">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> Congressional elections in 1890 had gone against the Republicans; and although Harrison had cooperated with congressional Republicans on legislation, several party leaders withdrew their support for him because of his adamant refusal to give party members the nod in the course of his executive appointments. Specifically, Thomas C. Platt, Matthew S. Quay, Thomas B. Reed and James Clarkson quietly organized the Grievance Committee, the ambition of which was to initiate a dump-Harrison offensive. They solicited the support of Blaine, without effect however, and Harrison in reaction resolved to run for re-election – seemingly forced to choose one of two options – "become a candidate or forever wear the name of a political coward".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter81_185-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter81-185">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It was clear that Harrison would not be re-nominated unanimously.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005134–137_186-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005134–137-186">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> Many of Harrison's detractors persisted in pushing for an incapacitated Blaine, though he announced that he was not a candidate in February 1892.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005134–137_186-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005134–137-186">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> Some party leaders still hoped to draft Blaine into running, and speculation increased when he resigned at the 11th hour as Secretary of State in June.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005138–139_187-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005138–139-187">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> At the <a href="/wiki/1892_Republican_National_Convention" title="1892 Republican National Convention">convention</a> in <a href="/wiki/Minneapolis" title="Minneapolis">Minneapolis</a>, Harrison prevailed on the first ballot, but encountered significant opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005140–141_188-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005140–141-188">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Democrats renominated former President Cleveland, making the 1892 election a rematch of the one four years earlier. The tariff revisions of the past four years had made imported goods so expensive that now many voters shifted to the reform position.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005147–150_189-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005147–150-189">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> Many westerners, traditionally Republican voters, defected to the new <a href="/wiki/People%27s_Party_(United_States)" title="People&#39;s Party (United States)">Populist Party</a> candidate, <a href="/wiki/James_B._Weaver" title="James B. Weaver">James Weaver</a>, who promised free silver, generous veterans' pensions, and an <a href="/wiki/Eight-hour_day" title="Eight-hour day">eight-hour work day</a>. The effects of the suppression of the <a href="/wiki/Homestead_Strike" class="mw-redirect" title="Homestead Strike">Homestead Strike</a> rebounded against the Republicans as well, although the federal government did not take action.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005145–147_190-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005145–147-190">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Harrison's wife Caroline began a critical struggle with <a href="/wiki/Tuberculosis" title="Tuberculosis">tuberculosis</a> earlier in 1892, and two weeks before the election, on October 25, she died from the disease.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005149_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005149-191">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> Their daughter <a href="/wiki/Mary_Harrison_McKee" title="Mary Harrison McKee">Mary Harrison McKee</a> assumed the role of <a href="/wiki/First_Lady_of_the_United_States" title="First Lady of the United States">First Lady</a> after her mother's death. Mrs. Harrison's terminal illness and the fact that both candidates had served in the White House called for a low key campaign, and resulted in neither of the candidates actively campaigning personally.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter198–199_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter198–199-192">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Cleveland ultimately won the election by 277 electoral votes to Harrison's 145, and also won the popular vote by 5,556,918 to 5,176,108; this was the most decisive presidential election in 20 years.<sup id="cite_ref-EV_1892_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EV_1892-193">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter199_194-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetter199-194">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> It gave Harrison the distinction of being the only president whose predecessor and successor were the same man.
</p>
<h2><span id="Post-presidency_.281893.E2.80.931901.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-presidency_(1893–1901)">Post-presidency (1893–1901)</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Grave_of_President_Benjamin_Harrison_and_his_two_wives_in_Indianapolis,_Indiana.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Grave_of_President_Benjamin_Harrison_and_his_two_wives_in_Indianapolis%2C_Indiana.jpg/170px-Grave_of_President_Benjamin_Harrison_and_his_two_wives_in_Indianapolis%2C_Indiana.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Grave_of_President_Benjamin_Harrison_and_his_two_wives_in_Indianapolis%2C_Indiana.jpg/255px-Grave_of_President_Benjamin_Harrison_and_his_two_wives_in_Indianapolis%2C_Indiana.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Grave_of_President_Benjamin_Harrison_and_his_two_wives_in_Indianapolis%2C_Indiana.jpg/340px-Grave_of_President_Benjamin_Harrison_and_his_two_wives_in_Indianapolis%2C_Indiana.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1536" data-file-height="2048" /></a><figcaption>Grave of President Harrison and his two wives in Indianapolis, Indiana</figcaption></figure>
<p>After he left office, Harrison visited the <a href="/wiki/World%27s_Columbian_Exposition" title="World&#39;s Columbian Exposition">World's Columbian Exposition</a> in Chicago in June 1893.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006150_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006150-195">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> After the Expo, Harrison returned to his home in Indianapolis. Harrison had been elected a companion of the <a href="/wiki/Military_Order_of_the_Loyal_Legion_of_the_United_States" title="Military Order of the Loyal Legion of the United States">Military Order of the Loyal Legion of the United States</a> in 1882, and was elected as commander (president) of the Ohio Commandery on May 3, 1893. For a few months in 1894, Harrison lived in San Francisco, California, where he gave law lectures at <a href="/wiki/Stanford_University" title="Stanford University">Stanford University</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005158_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005158-196">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> In 1896, some of Harrison's friends in the Republican party tried to convince him to seek the presidency again, but he declined. He traveled around the nation making appearances and speeches in support of William McKinley's candidacy for president.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005160–161_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005160–161-197">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From June 1895 to March 1901 Harrison served on the Board of Trustees of <a href="/wiki/Purdue_University" title="Purdue University">Purdue University</a>, where Harrison Hall, a dormitory, was named in his honor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006150_195-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006150-195">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> He wrote a series of articles about the federal government and the presidency which were republished in 1897 as a book titled <i>This Country of Ours</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Harrison_1897_198-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harrison_1897-198">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> In 1896, Harrison at age 62 remarried, to <a href="/wiki/Mary_Dimmick_Harrison" title="Mary Dimmick Harrison">Mary Scott Lord Dimmick</a>, the widowed 37-year-old niece and former secretary of his deceased wife. Harrison's two adult children, Russell, 41 years old at the time, and Mary (Mamie) McKee, 38, disapproved of the marriage and did not attend the wedding. Benjamin and Mary had one child together, <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_Harrison_Walker" title="Elizabeth Harrison Walker">Elizabeth</a> (February 21, 1897&#160;– December 26, 1955).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006153_199-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006153-199">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1898, Harrison served as an attorney for the Republic of Venezuela in their <a href="/wiki/Schomburgk_Line" class="mw-redirect" title="Schomburgk Line">British Guiana boundary dispute</a> with the United Kingdom.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006155_200-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006155-200">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> An international trial was agreed upon; he filed an 800-page brief and traveled to <a href="/wiki/Paris" title="Paris">Paris</a> where he spent more than 25 hours in court on Venezuela's behalf. Although he lost the case, his legal arguments won him international renown.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005160–163_201-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005160–163-201">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> In 1899 Harrison attended the <a href="/wiki/Hague_Convention_of_1899" class="mw-redirect" title="Hague Convention of 1899">First Peace Conference</a> at <a href="/wiki/The_Hague" title="The Hague">The Hague</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:2015-06-02_1758_Special_committee_on_creed_division_with_photo_of_Benjamin_Harrison_and_Judge_Edward_William_Cornelius_Humphrey.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/2015-06-02_1758_Special_committee_on_creed_division_with_photo_of_Benjamin_Harrison_and_Judge_Edward_William_Cornelius_Humphrey.png/220px-2015-06-02_1758_Special_committee_on_creed_division_with_photo_of_Benjamin_Harrison_and_Judge_Edward_William_Cornelius_Humphrey.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="108" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/2015-06-02_1758_Special_committee_on_creed_division_with_photo_of_Benjamin_Harrison_and_Judge_Edward_William_Cornelius_Humphrey.png/330px-2015-06-02_1758_Special_committee_on_creed_division_with_photo_of_Benjamin_Harrison_and_Judge_Edward_William_Cornelius_Humphrey.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/2015-06-02_1758_Special_committee_on_creed_division_with_photo_of_Benjamin_Harrison_and_Judge_Edward_William_Cornelius_Humphrey.png/440px-2015-06-02_1758_Special_committee_on_creed_division_with_photo_of_Benjamin_Harrison_and_Judge_Edward_William_Cornelius_Humphrey.png 2x" data-file-width="514" data-file-height="252" /></a><figcaption>Presbyterian General Assembly special committee on creed revision, including Benjamin Harrison and Judge Edward William Cornelius Humphrey</figcaption></figure>
<p>Harrison was an active <a href="/wiki/Presbyterianism" title="Presbyterianism">Presbyterian</a> and served as an Elder in the First Presbyterian Church of Indianapolis and on a special committee on creed revision in the national <a href="/wiki/Presbyterian_General_Assembly" class="mw-redirect" title="Presbyterian General Assembly">Presbyterian General Assembly</a>. However, he died before he could cast his vote at the meeting.<sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span></h3>
<p><span class="anchor" id="Death"></span>
Harrison developed what was thought to be <a href="/wiki/Influenza" title="Influenza">influenza</a> (then referred to as grippe), which later proved to be pneumonia, in February 1901. He was treated with steam vapor inhalation and oxygen, but his condition worsened. Harrison died from <a href="/wiki/Pneumonia" title="Pneumonia">pneumonia</a> at <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Harrison_Home" class="mw-redirect" title="Benjamin Harrison Home">his home</a> in Indianapolis on March 13, 1901, at the age of 67. His last words were reported to be, "Are the doctors here? Doctor, my lungs...". Harrison's remains are interred in Indianapolis's <a href="/wiki/Crown_Hill_Cemetery" title="Crown Hill Cemetery">Crown Hill Cemetery</a>, next to the remains of his first wife, Caroline. After her death in 1948, Mary Dimmick Harrison, his second wife, was buried beside him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006156_206-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMooreHale2006156-206">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Historical_reputation_and_memorials">Historical reputation and memorials</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Estatua_de_Benjamin_Harrison,_Indian%C3%A1polis,_Estados_Unidos,_2012-10-22,_DD_01.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/Estatua_de_Benjamin_Harrison%2C_Indian%C3%A1polis%2C_Estados_Unidos%2C_2012-10-22%2C_DD_01.jpg/220px-Estatua_de_Benjamin_Harrison%2C_Indian%C3%A1polis%2C_Estados_Unidos%2C_2012-10-22%2C_DD_01.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="108" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/Estatua_de_Benjamin_Harrison%2C_Indian%C3%A1polis%2C_Estados_Unidos%2C_2012-10-22%2C_DD_01.jpg/330px-Estatua_de_Benjamin_Harrison%2C_Indian%C3%A1polis%2C_Estados_Unidos%2C_2012-10-22%2C_DD_01.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/Estatua_de_Benjamin_Harrison%2C_Indian%C3%A1polis%2C_Estados_Unidos%2C_2012-10-22%2C_DD_01.jpg/440px-Estatua_de_Benjamin_Harrison%2C_Indian%C3%A1polis%2C_Estados_Unidos%2C_2012-10-22%2C_DD_01.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4119" data-file-height="2018" /></a><figcaption>Benjamin Harrison Statue, Indianapolis, Indiana</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Victory_Portraits_of_Benjamin_Harrison_and_Levi_P._Morton,_from_Judge.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b5/Victory_Portraits_of_Benjamin_Harrison_and_Levi_P._Morton%2C_from_Judge.jpg/220px-Victory_Portraits_of_Benjamin_Harrison_and_Levi_P._Morton%2C_from_Judge.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b5/Victory_Portraits_of_Benjamin_Harrison_and_Levi_P._Morton%2C_from_Judge.jpg/330px-Victory_Portraits_of_Benjamin_Harrison_and_Levi_P._Morton%2C_from_Judge.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b5/Victory_Portraits_of_Benjamin_Harrison_and_Levi_P._Morton%2C_from_Judge.jpg/440px-Victory_Portraits_of_Benjamin_Harrison_and_Levi_P._Morton%2C_from_Judge.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3000" data-file-height="1997" /></a><figcaption>Victory Portraits of Benjamin Harrison and Levi P. Morton for the 1888 election, from <i><a href="/wiki/Judge_(magazine)" title="Judge (magazine)">Judge</a></i></figcaption></figure>
<p>Historian Charles Calhoun gives Harrison major credit for innovative legislation in antitrust, monetary policy and tariffs. Historians have often given Secretary of State Blaine credit for foreign-policy initiatives. However, Calhoun argues that Harrison was even more responsible for the success of trade negotiations, the buildup of the steel Navy, overseas expansion, and emphasis on the American role in dominating the <a href="/wiki/Western_Hemisphere" title="Western Hemisphere">Western Hemisphere</a> through the <a href="/wiki/Monroe_Doctrine" title="Monroe Doctrine">Monroe Doctrine</a>. The major weakness which Calhoun sees was that the public and indeed the grassroots Republican Party was not fully prepared for this onslaught of major activity. The Democrats scored a sweeping landslide in 1890 by attacking the flagship legislation, especially the McKinley tariff, because it would raise the cost of living of the average American family. McKinley himself was defeated for reelection.<sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to historian R. Hal Williams, Harrison had a "widespread reputation for personal and official integrity". Closely scrutinized by Democrats, Harrison's reputation was largely intact when he left the White House. Having an advantage few 19th-century presidents had, Harrison's own party, the Republicans, controlled Congress, while his administration actively advanced a Republican program of a higher tariff, moderate control of corporations, protecting <a href="/wiki/African_American" class="mw-redirect" title="African American">African American</a> voting rights, a generous Civil War pension, and compromising over the controversial <a href="/wiki/Bimetallism" title="Bimetallism">silver issue</a>. Historians have not raised "serious questions about Harrison's own integrity or the integrity of his administration". <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilliams191_209-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams191-209">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the <a href="/wiki/Panic_of_1893" title="Panic of 1893">Panic of 1893</a>, Harrison became more popular in retirement.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun20056_210-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun20056-210">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup> Scholars have argued that Harrison's economic policies contributed to the Panic of 1893.<sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup> His legacy among historians is scant, and "general accounts of his period inaccurately treat Harrison as a cipher".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetterx_212-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetterx-212">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> More recently,
</p>
<blockquote><p>historians have recognized the importance of the Harrison administration—and Harrison himself—in the new foreign policy of the late nineteenth century. The administration faced challenges throughout the hemisphere, in the Pacific, and in relations with the European powers, involvements that would be taken for granted in the twenty first century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetterx_212-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESocolofsky_&amp;_Spetterx-212">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Harrison's presidency belongs properly to the 19th century, but he "clearly pointed the way" to the modern presidency that would emerge under <a href="/wiki/William_McKinley" title="William McKinley">William McKinley</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005166_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2005166-213">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> The bipartisan <a href="/wiki/Sherman_Anti-Trust_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Sherman Anti-Trust Act">Sherman Anti-Trust Act</a> signed into law by Harrison remains in effect over 120 years later and was the most important legislation passed by the Fifty-first Congress. Harrison's support for <a href="/wiki/African_American" class="mw-redirect" title="African American">African American</a> voting rights and education would be the last significant attempts to protect <a href="/wiki/Civil_and_political_rights" title="Civil and political rights">civil rights</a> until the 1930s. Harrison's tenacity at foreign policy was emulated by politicians such as <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt" title="Theodore Roosevelt">Theodore Roosevelt</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBatten209_214-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBatten209-214">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Benjamin_Harrison_1903_Issue-13c.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/Benjamin_Harrison_1903_Issue-13c.jpg/170px-Benjamin_Harrison_1903_Issue-13c.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="201" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/Benjamin_Harrison_1903_Issue-13c.jpg/255px-Benjamin_Harrison_1903_Issue-13c.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/Benjamin_Harrison_1903_Issue-13c.jpg/340px-Benjamin_Harrison_1903_Issue-13c.jpg 2x" data-file-width="610" data-file-height="720" /></a><figcaption><div class="center" style="width:auto; margin-left:auto; margin-right:auto;">The 1st Harrison stamp</div><div class="center" style="width:auto; margin-left:auto; margin-right:auto;">Issue of 1902</div></figcaption></figure>
<p>Harrison was memorialized on several postage stamps. The first was a 13-cent stamp issued on November 18, 1902, with the engraved likeness of Harrison modeled after a photo provided by his widow.<sup id="cite_ref-arago_215-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-arago-215">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> In all Harrison has been honored on <a href="/wiki/US_Presidents_on_US_postage_stamps#Benjamin_Harrison" class="mw-redirect" title="US Presidents on US postage stamps">six U.S. Postage stamps</a>, more than most other U.S. Presidents. Harrison also was featured on the five-dollar <a href="/wiki/National_Bank_Notes" class="mw-redirect" title="National Bank Notes">National Bank Notes</a> from the third charter period, beginning in 1902.<sup id="cite_ref-Hudgeons_Hudgeons_2000_216-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hudgeons_Hudgeons_2000-216">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> In 2012, a dollar coin with his image, part of the <a href="/wiki/Presidential_$1_Coin_Program" class="mw-redirect" title="Presidential $1 Coin Program">Presidential $1 Coin Program</a>, was issued.<sup id="cite_ref-US_Mint_dollar_coin_schedule_217-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-US_Mint_dollar_coin_schedule-217">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1908, the people of <a href="/wiki/Indianapolis,_Indiana" class="mw-redirect" title="Indianapolis, Indiana">Indianapolis</a> erected the <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Harrison_(Niehaus)" title="Benjamin Harrison (Niehaus)">Benjamin Harrison memorial statue</a>, created by Charles Niehaus and Henry Bacon, in honor of Harrison's lifetime achievements as military leader, U.S. Senator, and President of the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-INgov_218-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-INgov-218">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> The statue occupies a site on the south edge of <a href="/wiki/Indiana_World_War_Memorial_Plaza#University_Park" title="Indiana World War Memorial Plaza">University Park</a>, facing the <a href="/wiki/Birch_Bayh_Federal_Building_and_United_States_Courthouse" title="Birch Bayh Federal Building and United States Courthouse">Birch Bayh Federal Building and United States Courthouse</a> across New York Avenue.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreiff173_219-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreiff173-219">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1951, <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Harrison_Home" class="mw-redirect" title="Benjamin Harrison Home">Harrison's home</a> was opened to the public as a library and museum. It had been used as a dormitory for a music school from 1937 to 1950.<sup id="cite_ref-NPS_Benjamin_Harrison_Home_220-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NPS_Benjamin_Harrison_Home-220">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup> The house was designated as a <a href="/wiki/National_Historic_Landmark" title="National Historic Landmark">National Historic Landmark</a> in 1964.<sup id="cite_ref-Benjamin_Harrison_Presidential_Site_221-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Benjamin_Harrison_Presidential_Site-221">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Theodore Roosevelt dedicated <a href="/wiki/Fort_Benjamin_Harrison" title="Fort Benjamin Harrison">Fort Benjamin Harrison</a> in the former president's honor in 1906. It is located in <a href="/wiki/Lawrence,_Indiana" title="Lawrence, Indiana">Lawrence, Indiana</a>, a northeastern suburb of Indianapolis. The federal government decommissioned Fort Harrison in 1991 and transferred 1,700 of its 2,500 acres to Indiana's state government in 1995 to establish <a href="/wiki/Fort_Harrison_State_Park" title="Fort Harrison State Park">Fort Harrison State Park</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEConn94_222-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEConn94-222">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup> The site has been redeveloped to include residential neighborhoods and a golf course.
</p><p>In 1931, Franklin Hall at <a href="/wiki/Miami_University" title="Miami University">Miami University</a>, Harrison's alma mater, was renamed Harrison Hall. It was replaced by a <a href="/wiki/Harrison_Hall" title="Harrison Hall">new building of the same name</a> in 1960 and houses the college's political science department. In 1966, <a href="/wiki/Purdue_University" title="Purdue University">Purdue University</a> opened Harrison Hall, an eight-floor, 400-room residence hall. Harrison served as a Purdue University Trustee for the last six years of his life.<sup id="cite_ref-223" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-223">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<li id="cite_note-39"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-39">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite class="citation web cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://bhpsite.org/learn/benjamin-harrison/the-civil-war/">"The Civil War"</a>. <i>Benjamin Harrison Presidential Site</i><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">March 12,</span> 2022</span>. <q>In May 1864, Colonel Harrison and the regiment joined General Sherman's Atlanta campaign in the Army of the Cumberland.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.jtitle=Benjamin+Harrison+Presidential+Site&amp;rft.atitle=The+Civil+War&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbhpsite.org%2Flearn%2Fbenjamin-harrison%2Fthe-civil-war%2F&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABenjamin+Harrison" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun200536–44Wallace1888209–225-40"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun200536–44Wallace1888209–225_40-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalhoun200536–44Wallace1888209–225_40-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFCalhoun2005">Calhoun 2005</a>, pp.&#160;36–44; <a href="#CITEREFWallace1888">Wallace 1888</a>, pp.&#160;209–225.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-:1-41"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-:1_41-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite class="citation web cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://bhpsite.org/learn/benjamin-harrison/the-civil-war/">"The Civil War"</a>. <i>Benjamin Harrison Presidential Site</i><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">March 11,</span> 2022</span>. <q>Harrison's reputation became that of a strong leader. He earned the respect of his men and did not leave them in battle...Mr. Richard Smock remembered an incident while they were camped near Nashville during a very cold winter. Men on the picket line were nearly frozen to death, and Colonel Harrison fixed coffee and took it to them in the middle of the night. Harrison always led the men saying "Come on, boys!" as he took the lead.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.jtitle=Benjamin+Harrison+Presidential+Site&amp;rft.atitle=The+Civil+War&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbhpsite.org%2Flearn%2Fbenjamin-harrison%2Fthe-civil-war%2F&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABenjamin+Harrison" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-:0-42"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-:0_42-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-:0_42-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-:0_42-2"><sup><i><b>c</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-:0_42-3"><sup><i><b>d</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite class="citation web cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.georgiaencyclopedia.org/articles/history-archaeology/battle-of-resaca/">"Battle of Resaca"</a>. <i>New Georgia Encyclopedia</i><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">March 11,</span> 2022</span>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.jtitle=New+Georgia+Encyclopedia&amp;rft.atitle=Battle+of+Resaca&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.georgiaencyclopedia.org%2Farticles%2Fhistory-archaeology%2Fbattle-of-resaca%2F&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABenjamin+Harrison" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-:2-43"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-:2_43-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-:2_43-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-:2_43-2"><sup><i><b>c</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-:2_43-3"><sup><i><b>d</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite class="citation web cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://bhpsite.org/learn/benjamin-harrison/the-civil-war/">"The Civil War"</a>. <i>Benjamin Harrison Presidential Site</i><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">March 11,</span> 2022</span>. <q>Brigadier General Ward's brigade, of which Harrison was a part, was ordered to assault and capture this redoubt. The brigade attacked in a column formation, the 70th having the honor of leading the charge. The redoubt was heavily fortified with three infantry regiments in the rifle pits and four more regiments in the main trenches...Harrison captured the battery in hand-to-hand fighting with the gunners. Fierce fighting continued all afternoon. At nightfall, the 70th carried the four captured 121-pound Napoleon Cannons to the rear</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.jtitle=Benjamin+Harrison+Presidential+Site&amp;rft.atitle=The+Civil+War&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbhpsite.org%2Flearn%2Fbenjamin-harrison%2Fthe-civil-war%2F&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABenjamin+Harrison" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-44"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-44">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite class="citation web cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.battlefields.org/learn/articles/missed-opportunity">"A Missed Opportunity"</a>. <i>American Battlefield Trust</i>. September 30, 2014<span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">March 12,</span> 2022</span>. <q>Thirty-three years old in 1864, Harrison led his brigade forward and helped stop the fierce assault made by Featherston's Mississippi brigade...Featherston's Mississippi brigade charged up and over Collier Road and engaged future United States President Benjamin Harrison's Union brigade.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.jtitle=American+Battlefield+Trust&amp;rft.atitle=A+Missed+Opportunity&amp;rft.date=2014-09-30&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.battlefields.org%2Flearn%2Farticles%2Fmissed-opportunity&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABenjamin+Harrison" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-45"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-45">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite class="citation web cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://ironbrigader.com/2017/07/16/future-president-benjamin-harrisons-report-brigade-battle-peachtree-creek/">"Future President Benjamin Harrison's Report on His Brigade at the Battle of Peachtree Creek"</a>. July 16, 2017<span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">March 17,</span> 2022</span>. <q>Harrison now found himself in command of a brigade consisting of the 102nd, 105th, and 129th Illinois Infantry regiments, plus the 79th Ohio Infantry and his own 70th Indiana. The brigade was deployed roughly in the center of the Union line and was heavily engaged with Major General William W. Loring's division of Mississippi and Alabama troops of Stewart's Corps.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.btitle=Future+President+Benjamin+Harrison%27s+Report+on+His+Brigade+at+the+Battle+of+Peachtree+Creek&amp;rft.date=2017-07-16&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fironbrigader.com%2F2017%2F07%2F16%2Ffuture-president-benjamin-harrisons-report-brigade-battle-peachtree-creek%2F&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABenjamin+Harrison" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-46"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-46">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite class="citation web cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://ironbrigader.com/2017/07/16/future-president-benjamin-harrisons-report-brigade-battle-peachtree-creek/">"Future President Benjamin Harrison's Report on His Brigade at the Battle of Peachtree Creek"</a>. July 16, 2017<span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">March 17,</span> 2022</span>. <q>HDQRS. FIRST BRIG., THIRD DIV., TWENTIETH CORPS, Before Atlanta, Ga., August 12, 1864...At one time during the fight our ammunition began to get low and considerable uneasiness was felt lest it might be exhausted. I at once dispatched Lieutenant Mitchell, aide-de-camp, to have a supply brought up, while Captain Scott, acting assistant inspector-general, and others busied themselves in cutting the cartridge-boxes from the rebel dead within our lines and distributing them to the men.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.btitle=Future+President+Benjamin+Harrison%27s+Report+on+His+Brigade+at+the+Battle+of+Peachtree+Creek&amp;rft.date=2017-07-16&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fironbrigader.com%2F2017%2F07%2F16%2Ffuture-president-benjamin-harrisons-report-brigade-battle-peachtree-creek%2F&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABenjamin+Harrison" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>

<li id="cite_note-92"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-92">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite class="citation web cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.presidency.ucsb.edu/documents/republican-party-platform-1888">"Republican Party Platform of 1888 | The American Presidency Project"</a>. <i>www.presidency.ucsb.edu</i><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">March 18,</span> 2022</span>. <q>We are uncompromisingly in favor of the American system of protection; we protest against its destruction as proposed by the President and his party. They serve the interests of Europe; we will support the interests of America. We accept the issue, and confidently appeal to the people for their judgment. The protective system must be maintained. Its abandonment has always been followed by general disaster to all interests, except those of the usurer and the sheriff. We denounce the Mills bill as destructive to the general business, the labor and the farming interests of the country, and we heartily indorse the consistent and patriotic action of the Republican Representatives in Congress in opposing its passage.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.jtitle=www.presidency.ucsb.edu&amp;rft.atitle=Republican+Party+Platform+of+1888+%7C+The+American+Presidency+Project&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.presidency.ucsb.edu%2Fdocuments%2Frepublican-party-platform-1888&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABenjamin+Harrison" class="Z3988"></span></span>
